{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Conclusion}\\n\\\\label{sec:conclusion}\\n\\nWe measure a lower bound on the generational depth discrimination boundary $\\\\Kempirical$ for prompt-chain recursive synthetic text.\\n$\\\\Kempirical \\\\leq 3$ across all ten configurations (six architecture families, 1B--8B) at $\\\\theta{=}0.60$; for general web text, $\\\\Kempirical \\\\leq 2$ regardless of model family or scale, while structured academic text (arXiv) reaches $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$ across two independent architectures.\\nAmong the six nominal $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ web text configurations, four retain significance under Bonferroni correction; the two marginal cases reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.\\nThis one-wash laundering effect means that a single generation pass renders web text statistically indistinguishable from deeper recursive content, concentrating all exploitable signal at the 0v1 boundary.\\n\\nThe practical consequence is direct: for web text, binary filtering (depth 0 vs.\\\\ $\\\\geq$1) captures the only reliable discrimination signal, and graduated weighting beyond $\\\\Kempirical$ injects noise from near-random depth assignments.\\nThe consistency check confirms this prediction, with binary curation most effectively minimizing MMLU degradation among six strategies tested ($-$0.77\\\\,pp vs.\\\\ $-$1.53\\\\,pp for no-filter; Section~\\\\ref{sec:filtering}).\\nFor structured domains where $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$, graduated strategies can exploit one additional depth level, so corpus curators should calibrate their filtering approach to the source domain rather than assuming a universal boundary.\\n\\nThe 15 statistical features enable deployment across generators without retraining: continuation-mode classifiers retain 80--92\\\\% accuracy across model families, and scorer-independent feature rankings ($\\\\rho > 0.90$) make the framework applicable to corpora generated by unknown or heterogeneous models.\\n$\\\\Kempirical$ converts a continuous AUC decay surface into a discrete operating boundary, replacing ad hoc depth thresholds with a measurement grounded in the Data Processing Inequality.\\nSeveral directions remain open: tracking $\\\\Kempirical$ as model scale surpasses 13B (where 0v1 JSD attenuation suggests $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ may become dominant), bridging the prompt-chain and retrain-chain boundaries to connect text-level measurement with the model collapse literature, and extending to additional structured domains and scorer-ensemble strategies.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.9}}"}

[System 触发审查]:
**论文整体叙事强度审视**

当前论文已完成 D104 narrative upgrade（universal-bound headline），请独立评估以下问题：

1. **Universal-bound framing 是否最优？** 当前 headline："K*≤3 all configs, ≤2 web text"。Bonferroni 修正后 4/10 per-config、0/6 per-family unanimous。这个 framing 是否足够强？有没有更好的叙事角度？

2. **Layered narrative 一致性**：one-wash laundering（hook）→ domain dependence（backbone）→ binary filtering（practical）。三层是否自洽？Conclusion 刚扩充到 16 行，新增内容（实践含义、领域校准、跨模型部署、retrain-chain gap、规模趋势）是否与主线叙事一致，还是散了？

3. **Contribution 定位**：当前定位是 "measurement framework + empirical finding"。对 EMNLP 2026（Overall 4.0 = Conference accept），这个贡献强度够不够？有没有被低估或高估的 claim？

4. **Reviewer 可能的攻击面**：以当前论文状态，最可能被 reviewer 挑战的 1-2 个点是什么？我们还有时间补强。

请基于论文当前状态（main.tex + 所有 section）做独立评估，不要只看 ROADMAP 摘要。

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"% EMNLP 2026 Main — ARR May 2026\\n% Generational Depth Contamination\\n\\\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\\n\\\\usepackage[review]{acl}\\n\\\\usepackage{times}\\n\\\\usepackage{latexsym}\\n\\\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\\n\\\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\\n\\\\usepackage{microtype}\\n\\\\usepackage{graphicx}\\n\\\\usepackage{amsmath}\\n\\\\usepackage{amssymb}\\n\\\\usepackage{booktabs}\\n\\\\usepackage{colortbl}\\n\\\\usepackage{multirow}\\n\\\\usepackage{enumitem}\\n\\\\usepackage{subcaption}\\n\\\\usepackage[section]{placeins}\\n\\n\\\\newcommand{\\\\Kstar}{{K^*}}\\n\\\\newcommand{\\\\Kempirical}{{K^*_{\\\\text{empirical}}}}\\n\\\\newcommand{\\\\Ktheory}{{K^*_{\\\\text{theory}}}}\\n\\\\newcommand{\\\\auc}{\\\\text{AUC}}\\n\\\\newcommand{\\\\jsd}{\\\\text{JSD}}\\n\\n\\\\title{Generational Depth Estimation of Recursive Synthetic Text:\\\\\\\\Measuring the Discrimination Boundary}\\n\\n\\\\author{Anonymous}\\n\\n\\\\begin{document}\\n\\\\maketitle\\n\\n% === Abstract ===\\n\\\\begin{abstract}\\nWeb-scale corpora are increasingly contaminated by recursive synthetic content, yet no method estimates a document's generational depth.\\nWe measure a lower bound on the generational discrimination boundary, $\\\\Kempirical$ (the maximum depth at which adjacent generations remain distinguishable), using 15 statistical features and an OBD-LightGBM classifier.\\nUnder prompt-chain generation across 10 model configurations (6 architecture families, 1B--8B), 3 source domains, and 4 decoding strategies, $\\\\Kempirical \\\\leq 3$ across all ten configurations (six families, 1B--8B) at $\\\\theta{=}0.60$; for web text (C4, Wikipedia), $\\\\Kempirical \\\\leq 2$ regardless of model family or scale, with structured academic text (arXiv) reaching $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$ across two architectures.\\nAmong the six nominal $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ configurations, four retain significance under Bonferroni correction; the two marginal cases reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.\\nA single generation pass suffices to render general web text indistinguishable from deeper synthetic content, a \\\\emph{one-wash laundering} effect.\\nModel-free statistical features enable 80--100\\\\% cross-model transfer with scorer-invariant feature rankings ($\\\\rho > 0.90$).\\nAs a consistency check, domain-appropriate $\\\\Kempirical$ correctly predicts that binary filtering outperforms graduated alternatives for web text when contamination causes detectable quality loss.\\n\\\\end{abstract}\\n\\n% === Section 1: Introduction ===\\n\\\\input{introduction}\\n\\n% === Section 2: Related Work ===\\n\\\\input{related_work}\\n\\n% === Section 3: Method ===\\n\\\\input{method}\\n\\n% === Section 4: Experiments ===\\n\\\\input{experiments_c1}\\n\\n% === Section 5: K*-Aware Data Filtering ===\\n\\\\input{experiments_c3}\\n\\n% === Section 6: Discussion ===\\n\\\\input{discussion}\\n\\n% === Section 7: Conclusion ===\\n\\\\input{conclusion}\\n\\n% === Limitations (ACL required, unnumbered) ===\\n\\\\clearpage\\n\\\\section*{Limitations}\\n\\nOur findings are bounded by:\\n(1)~\\\\emph{Scope}: model scale $\\\\leq$8B (6 architecture families), English text (C4, Wikipedia, arXiv), four decoding strategies; arXiv $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$ is confirmed with two model families (Qwen-1.5B, Pythia-1.4B) but with reduced sample size ($n{=}2{,}200$ per model).\\n(2)~\\\\emph{Protocol}: prompt-chain rather than retrain-chain. Retrain-chain validation (Pythia-1.4B LoRA, Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}) yields $\\\\Kempirical{\\\\geq}4$ due to cumulative model-adaptation artifacts; prompt-chain isolates text-intrinsic degradation and provides the operationally relevant boundary for data curation.\\n(3)~\\\\emph{Threshold}: $\\\\Kempirical$ ranges from 2--3 at $\\\\theta{=}0.55$ to 1 at $\\\\theta{=}0.70$; 6/10 configurations yield $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ at $\\\\theta{=}0.60$ (OLMo-1B, Mistral-7B, Gemma-2-2B, Pythia-6.9B: $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$).\\n(4)~\\\\emph{Marginal boundary}: Qwen-continuation's 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\\\\theta$ by only 0.002; cross-scorer validation drops this to $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ (Section~\\\\ref{sec:transfer}).\\n(5)~\\\\emph{Feature set}: the 15D features yield a feature-set-specific lower bound ($\\\\Ktheory$ may be strictly higher); neural probe $\\\\Kempirical$ is model-dependent ($3$ for Qwen, $2$ for Pythia, $1$ for OLMo).\\n(6)~\\\\emph{Segment length}: $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ confirmed for 128 and 256 tokens (CI lower $> \\\\theta$); 512 tokens yields point estimate $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ but CI lower marginally below $\\\\theta$ ($0.5979$), indicating the boundary weakens at longer generation lengths.\\nLonger documents may require larger sample sizes or more sensitive feature sets to maintain the boundary; whether this reflects genuine information-theoretic limits or finite-sample attenuation remains open.\\n(7)~\\\\emph{Curation applicability}: the practical benefit of $\\\\Kstar$-aware curation depends on contamination severity; when synthetic data proportion is low enough to leave overall training quality intact, filtering strategies converge (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:dose_response}).\\nOur filtering validation (Section~\\\\ref{sec:filtering}) was conducted exclusively on Qwen-1.5B continuation-mode text; extending to $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ generators remains future work.\\nSeed robustness testing (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:seed_robustness}) shows $\\\\Kempirical$ is stable under seed variation for strong-signal configurations, but marginal boundaries are seed-sensitive (absolute AUC shifts up to ${\\\\pm}9$\\\\,pp).\\nAdditional limitations (cross-model scoring sensitivity, feature ranking variability at the 1v2 boundary, structural composition confound analysis) appear in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.\\n\\n% === Ethics Statement (ACL required, unnumbered) ===\\n\\\\section*{Ethics Statement}\\n\\nGenerational depth estimation is intended for data curation and corpus auditing, positive-sum applications that improve training data quality.\\nOur experiments use the publicly available C4 corpus and do not involve personally identifiable information.\\nA potential dual-use concern is that depth estimation techniques could inform adversarial strategies to evade detection (e.g., by calibrating rewriting to mimic earlier-generation statistics); we note that this risk is shared by all detection research and that the binary detection boundary ($\\\\auc \\\\geq 0.96$ at 0v1) remains robust.\\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\\n\\n\\\\bibliography{references}\\n\\n\\\\input{appendix}\\n\\\\input{appendix_extended}\\n\\n\\\\end{document}\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.92}}"}
```

extending to $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ generators remains future work.\\nSeed robustness testing (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:seed_robustness}) shows $\\\\Kempirical$ is stable under seed variation for strong-signal configurations, but marginal boundaries are seed-sensitive (absolute AUC shifts up to ${\\\\pm}9$\\\\,pp).\\nAdditional limitations (cross-model scoring sensitivity, feature ranking variability at the 1v2 boundary, structural composition confound analysis) appear in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.\\n\\n% === Ethics Statement (ACL required, unnumbered) ===\\n\\\\section*{Ethics Statement}\\n\\nGenerational depth estimation is intended for data curation and corpus auditing, positive-sum applications that improve training data quality.\\nOur experiments use the publicly available C4 corpus and do not involve personally identifiable information.\\nA potential dual-use concern is that depth estimation techniques could inform adversarial strategies to evade detection (e.g., by calibrating rewriting to mimic earlier-generation statistics); we note that this risk is shared by all detection research and that the binary detection boundary ($\\\\auc \\\\geq 0.96$ at 0v1) remains robust.\\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\\n\\n\\\\bibliography{references}\\n\\n\\\\input{appendix}\\n\\\\input{appendix_extended}\\n\\n\\\\end{document}\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.92}}"

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Introduction}\\n\\\\label{sec:introduction}\\n\\nWeb-scale text corpora are increasingly contaminated by synthetic content that has itself been generated from earlier synthetic content.\\nEstimates suggest that a substantial fraction of online text is machine-generated~\\\\cite{sun2025aigt, spennemann2025quantifying}, and this share continues to rise.\\nWhen models train on the outputs of earlier models, the resulting multi-generational synthetic text degrades with each iteration: tail modes vanish, lexical diversity contracts, and surprisal distributions flatten~\\\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.\\nThis degradation arises in both \\\\emph{retrain-chains} (iteratively training on model outputs) and \\\\emph{prompt-chains} (recursively generating from previous outputs without retraining); we study the latter, which isolates generation-level information loss from training dynamics (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\\nYet the dominant paradigm treats all machine-generated text identically, discarding the generational depth dimension.\\n\\nThis gap cuts across three communities: binary detectors~\\\\cite{basani2026diveye, zhang2024minkpp} produce no depth estimate (Section~\\\\ref{sec:binary_confidence}); membership inference~\\\\cite{zawalski2026codec} addresses a different question; the model collapse literature~\\\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, alemohammad2024selfconsuming, schaeffer2025position} provides no per-text tool; and data selection methods~\\\\cite{xie2023dsir, tirumala2023d4, drayson2025} ignore recursive depth.\\nNo existing method estimates how many rounds of recursive generation a text has undergone.\\n\\nThe Data Processing Inequality guarantees that information degrades monotonically across generations, motivating a finite discrimination boundary $\\\\Kstar$.\\nWe empirically measure a lower bound $\\\\Kempirical$ on the discrimination boundary (the maximum depth at which a classifier reliably distinguishes adjacent generations) using 15 statistical features and an Ordinal Binary Decomposition (OBD) LightGBM classifier.\\nWe operationalize $\\\\Kempirical$ as the deepest $k \\\\geq 1$ where the pairwise $\\\\auc_{k-1,k}$ exceeds $\\\\theta = 0.60$ (Section~\\\\ref{sec:formulation}).\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure*}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\textwidth]{figures/pipeline.pdf}\\n\\\\caption{Generational depth estimation pipeline. Seed documents are recursively passed through a generation operator $G_\\\\delta$ to produce a depth chain. Corpus-level statistical features are fed to the OBD-LightGBM classifier for depth prediction.}\\n\\\\label{fig:pipeline}\\n\\\\end{figure*}\\n\\nOur contributions:\\n\\\\begin{enumerate}[label=(\\\\alph*),nosep]\\n  \\\\item \\\\textbf{$\\\\Kempirical$ as a measurement tool} across 10 model configurations (6 families, 1B--8B) and 4 decoding strategies ($p \\\\leq 0.002$; Section~\\\\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}):\\n  $\\\\Kempirical \\\\leq 3$ universally; for web text, $\\\\Kempirical \\\\leq 2$ regardless of family or scale.\\n  $\\\\Kempirical$ establishes binary sufficiency for web text by measurement (not assumption), reveals domain-dependent variation (arXiv $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$), and quantifies boundary sensitivity to threshold, scorer, and model scale.\\n  Both supervised OBD and zero-shot LLMs fail beyond $\\\\Kempirical$ (McNemar $p > 0.05$; Section~\\\\ref{sec:why_kstar}).\\n  \\\\item \\\\textbf{Domain dependence of $\\\\Kempirical$}: general web text (C4, Wikipedia) yields $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$, while structured academic text (arXiv) yields $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$, consistent across model families (Section~\\\\ref{sec:cross_domain}).\\n  \\\\item \\\\textbf{Model-free cross-model transfer}: 15 statistical features enable 80--100\\\\% accuracy retention with scorer-invariant feature rankings ($\\\\rho > 0.90$); $\\\\Kempirical$ agreement holds across scorers for strong-signal configurations (Section~\\\\ref{sec:transfer}).\\n\\\\end{enumerate}\\n\\n\\\\noindent As a consistency check, $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ correctly predicts the strategy ordering for web-text filtering: binary curation minimizes degradation most effectively (MMLU $0.5941$ vs.\\\\ base $0.6018$; Section~\\\\ref{sec:filtering}).\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.45}}"}

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Method}\\n\\\\label{sec:method}\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure*}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\textwidth]{figures/pipeline.pdf}\\n\\\\caption{Generational depth estimation pipeline. Seed documents are recursively passed through a generation operator $G_\\\\delta$ to produce a depth chain. Statistical features are fed to the OBD-LightGBM classifier for depth prediction.}\\n\\\\label{fig:pipeline}\\n\\\\end{figure*}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\\n\\\\label{sec:formulation}\\n\\nA generation operator $G_\\\\delta$ (LLM + decoding strategy $\\\\delta$) produces successive generations $X \\\\to Y_1 \\\\to \\\\cdots \\\\to Y_K$, where $Y_k = G_\\\\delta(Y_{k-1})$ and $Y_0 \\\\triangleq X$ (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\\nThis sequence forms a Markov chain.\\nBy the Data Processing Inequality, $I(X; Y_1) \\\\geq I(X; Y_2) \\\\geq \\\\cdots \\\\geq 0$: mutual information with the seed text degrades monotonically.\\nTogether with Markov chain convergence, this provides heuristic motivation for expecting $\\\\auc_{k-1,k}$ to decrease with $k$, though AUC and mutual information are not formally equivalent; the actual saturation depth must therefore be measured empirically.\\n\\nWe define the operational lower bound:\\n\\\\begin{equation}\\n  \\\\Kempirical = \\\\max\\\\!\\\\bigl\\\\{k \\\\!\\\\geq\\\\! 1 : \\\\auc_{j-1,j} > \\\\theta,\\\\;\\\\forall\\\\, j \\\\!\\\\leq\\\\! k\\\\bigr\\\\},\\n  \\\\label{eq:kstar}\\n\\\\end{equation}\\nwhich requires all pairs up to depth $k$ to exceed the threshold (the $\\\\forall j$ constraint is naturally satisfied because $\\\\auc_{k-1,k}$ decreases monotonically above $\\\\theta$ across all tested configurations; Table~\\\\ref{tab:kstar_ci}).\\nThe theoretical upper bound $\\\\Ktheory$ (the true maximum discriminable depth given optimal features) is not directly estimated in this work; $\\\\Kempirical$ provides a feature-set-specific lower bound on $\\\\Ktheory$.\\nHere $\\\\auc_{k-1,k}$ is the point-estimate pairwise AUC and $\\\\theta = 0.60$ is a small-effect-size threshold (Cohen's $h \\\\approx 0.20$\\\\footnote{$h = 2\\\\arcsin\\\\!\\\\sqrt{0.60} - 2\\\\arcsin\\\\!\\\\sqrt{0.50} \\\\approx 0.2014$, a ``small'' effect size by Cohen's (1988) convention.}; justified below).\\nBootstrap 95\\\\% CIs (1{,}000 resamples) provide uncertainty quantification: we verify that boundary-pair CI lower bounds exceed $\\\\theta$ and non-boundary-pair CI upper bounds fall below $\\\\theta$ (Section~\\\\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}).\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Threshold justification.}\\n$\\\\theta{=}0.60$ is supported by three independent lines of evidence.\\nFirst, it corresponds to Cohen's $h \\\\approx 0.20$, a conventional small-effect-size boundary.\\nSecond, a 10{,}000-iteration permutation test yields a 95th-percentile null of ${<}\\\\,0.55$ and $p < 0.002$ for all boundary comparisons, placing $\\\\theta{=}0.60$ well above the empirical null.\\nThird, as post-hoc confirmatory evidence, the conclusion $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ is insensitive to the exact threshold: 1v2 AUC values cluster at $0.61$--$0.70$ while 2v3 values cluster at $0.55$--$0.59$, creating a ${\\\\sim}$5--11\\\\,pp discontinuity that any $\\\\theta \\\\in [0.55, 0.65]$ would separate (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:kstar_theta}; Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\\nOnly at extremes does $\\\\Kempirical$ shift: $\\\\theta{=}0.55$ yields $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$ for two Qwen configurations; $\\\\theta{=}0.70$ yields $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ across all configurations.\\nThe downstream filtering evaluation (Section~\\\\ref{sec:filtering}) uses the OBD classifier without re-optimizing $\\\\theta$, avoiding circularity.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Features and Classifier}\\n\\\\label{sec:features}\\n\\nEach document is characterized by a 15-dimensional feature vector spanning four groups: \\\\emph{surprisal moments} (mean, std, skewness, kurtosis; 4~dims), \\\\emph{surprisal derivatives} (mean and std of first- and second-order differences, plus first-order skewness; 5~dims), \\\\emph{vocabulary diversity} (type-token ratio, hapax legomena ratio, self-BLEU; 3~dims), and \\\\emph{frequency tail statistics} (frequency kurtosis, entropy, rare token fraction; 3~dims).\\nAll features are computed from token-level surprisal values via an external scorer model (full definitions in Table~\\\\ref{tab:s5_features}, Appendix).\\nSelf-BLEU is computed within each CV fold using only same-fold, same-depth reference samples to prevent leakage; it ranks \\\\#1 in 1/8 conditions (greedy decoding) but is absent from the top-3 in all 7 others.\\nRemoving self-BLEU reduces mean $\\\\auc_{1v2}$ by $< 1$\\\\,pp across all models (14D $\\\\Kempirical = 2$ for all 4 configurations); in deployment where depth labels are unavailable, self-BLEU can be replaced with corpus-level self-similarity or omitted with minimal performance loss.\\nHand-crafted features enable cross-generator transfer without retraining and permit attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties.\\n\\nWe use \\\\emph{Ordinal Binary Decomposition} (OBD), decomposing the $(K{+}1)$-class problem into $K$ cumulative binary sub-classifiers, each estimating $P(\\\\text{depth} > k \\\\mid \\\\mathbf{x})$ via LightGBM (hyperparameters in Table~\\\\ref{tab:s6_lgbm_params}).\\nOBD achieves lower MAE than multiclass LightGBM (e.g., 0.984 vs.\\\\ 1.016 on Qwen-rewrite) while maintaining superior pairwise $\\\\auc$ (mean 0.6095 vs.\\\\ 0.4883 for ordinal logistic regression).\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\\n\\\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\\n\\nWe evaluate six configurations spanning three model families (Table~\\\\ref{tab:setup}): Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (rewrite and continuation modes), Qwen2.5-1.5B (base, continuation), Qwen2.5-7B (continuation), Pythia-1.4B and OLMo-1B (continuation).\\nEach uses 5{,}000 C4 seeds truncated to 256 tokens, recursively generated for $K{=}5$ iterations (30{,}000 documents per configuration).\\nDefault decoding: nucleus $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$; decoding ablation covers greedy, $T{=}0.7$, and $T{=}1.2$.\\nContinuation mode truncates seeds to 128 tokens and extends to 256; rewrite mode paraphrases the full text.\\nEvaluation: 5-fold GroupKFold cross-validation grouped by seed document; pairwise $\\\\auc$ as primary metric.\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\caption{Experimental configurations for depth-chain generation. \\\\textbf{Config}: shorthand identifier; \\\\textbf{Model}: architecture and parameter count; \\\\textbf{Mode}: generation strategy (continuation or rewrite); $N$: number of seed documents per depth level.}\\n\\\\label{tab:setup}\\n\\\\resizebox{\\\\columnwidth}{!}{%\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\\n\\\\toprule\\n\\\\textbf{Config} & \\\\textbf{Model} & \\\\textbf{Mode} & $N$ \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nQwen-rw   & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Rewrite & 5{,}000 \\\\\\\\\\nQwen-cont & Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\\\\\\nQwen-base & Qwen2.5-1.5B          & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\\\\\\nPythia    & Pythia-1.4B            & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\\\\\\nOLMo      & OLMo-1B               & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\\\\\\nQwen-7B   & Qwen2.5-7B            & Continuation & 5{,}000 \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.45}}"}

Table~\\\\\\\\ref{tab:kstar_ci}).\\\\nThe theoretical upper bound $\\\\\\\\Ktheory$ (the true maximum discriminable depth given optimal features) is not directly estimated in this work; $\\\\\\\\Kempirical$ provides a feature-set-specific lower bound on $\\\\\\\\Ktheory$.\\\\nHere $\\\\\\\\auc_{k-1,k}$ is the point-estimate pairwise AUC and $\\\\\\\\theta = 0.60$ is a small-effect-size threshold (Cohen's $h \\\\\\\\approx 0.20$\\\\\\\\footnote{$h = 2\\\\\\\\arcsin\\\\\\\\!\\\\\\\\sqrt{0.60} - 2\\\\\\\\arcsin\\\\\\\\!\\\\\\\\sqrt{0.50} \\\\\\\\approx 0.2014$, a ``small'' effect size by Cohen's (1988) convention.}; justified below).\\\\nBootstrap 95\\\\\\\\% CIs (1{,}000 resamples) provide uncertainty quantification: we verify that boundary-pair CI lower bounds exceed $\\\\\\\\theta$ and non-boundary-pair CI upper bounds fall below $\\\\\\\\theta$ (Section~\\\\\\\\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}).\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\paragraph{Threshold justification.}\\\\n$\\\\\\\\theta{=}0.60$ is supported by three independent lines of evidence.\\\\nFirst, it corresponds to Cohen's $h \\\\\\\\approx 0.20$, a conventional small-effect-size boundary.\\\\nSecond, a 10{,}000-iteration permutation test yields a 95th-percentile null of ${<}\\\\\\\\,0.55$ and $p < 0.002$ for all boundary comparisons, placing $\\\\\\\\theta{=}0.60$ well above the empirical null.\\\\nThird, as post-hoc confirmatory evidence, the conclusion $\\\\\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ is insensitive to the exact threshold: 1v2 AUC values cluster at $0.61$--$0.70$ while 2v3 values cluster at $0.55$--$0.59$, creating a ${\\\\\\\\sim}$5--11\\\\\\\\,pp discontinuity that any $\\\\\\\\theta \\\\\\\\in [0.55, 0.65]$ would separate (Figure~\\\\\\\\ref{fig:kstar_theta}; Appendix~\\\\\\\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\\\\nOnly at extremes does $\\\\\\\\Kempirical$ shift: $\\\\\\\\theta{=}0.55$ yields $\\\\\\\\Kempirical{=}3$ for two Qwen configurations; $\\\\\\\\theta{=}0.70$ yields $\\\\\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ across all configurations.\\\\nThe downstream filtering evaluation (Section~\\\\\\\\ref{sec:filtering}) uses the OBD classifier without re-optimizing $\\\\\\\\theta$, avoiding circularity.\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\subsection{Features and Classifier}\\\\n\\\\\\\\label{sec:features}\\\\n\\\\nEach document is characterized by a 15-dimensional feature vector spanning four groups: \\\\\\\\emph{surprisal moments} (mean, std, skewness, kurtosis; 4~dims), \\\\\\\\emph{surprisal derivatives} (mean and std of first- and second-order differences, plus first-order skewness; 5~dims), \\\\\\\\emph{vocabulary diversity} (type-token ratio, hapax legomena ratio, self-BLEU; 3~dims), and \\\\\\\\emph{frequency tail statistics} (frequency kurtosis, entropy, rare token fraction; 3~dims).\\\\nAll features are computed from token-level surprisal values via an external scorer model (full definitions in Table~\\\\\\\\ref{tab:s5_features}, Appendix).\\\\nSelf-BLEU is computed within each CV fold using only same-fold, same-depth reference samples to prevent leakage; it ranks \\\\\\\\#1 in 1/8 conditions (greedy decoding) but is absent from the top-3 in all 7 others.\\\\nRemoving self-BLEU reduces mean $\\\\\\\\auc_{1v2}$ by $< 1$\\\\\\\\,pp across all models (14D $\\\\\\\\Kempirical = 2$ for all 4 configurations); in deployment where depth labels are unavailable, self-BLEU can be replaced with corpus-level self-similarity or omitted with minimal performance loss.\\\\nHand-crafted features enable cross-generator transfer without retraining and permit attribution of discriminative power to specific text properties.\\\\n\\\\nWe use \\\\\\\\emph{Ordinal Binary Decomposition} (OBD), decomposing the $(K{+}1)$-class problem into $K$ cumulative binary sub-classifiers, each estimating $P(\\\\\\\\text{depth} > k \\\\\\\\mid \\\\\\\\mathbf{x})$ via LightGBM (hyperparameters in Table~\\\\\\\\ref{tab:s6_lgbm_params}).\\\\nOBD achieves lower MAE than multiclass LightGBM (e.g., 0.984 vs.\\\\\\\\ 1.016 on Qwen-rewrite) while maintaining superior pairwise $\\\\\\\\auc$ (mean 0.6095 vs.\\\\\\\\ 0.4883 for ordinal logistic regression).\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\\\\n\\\\\\\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\\\\n\\\\nWe evaluate six configurations spanning three model families (Table~\\\\\\\\ref{tab:setup}): Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (rewrite and continuation modes), Qwen2.5-1.5B (base, continuation), Qwen2.5-7B (continuation), Pythia-1.4B and OLMo-1B (continuation).\\\\nEach uses 5{,}000 C4 seeds truncated to 256 tokens, recursively generated for $K{=}5$ iterations (30{,}000 documents per configuration).\\\\nDefault decoding: nucleus $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$; Each cell reports AUC for classifying depth $d$ vs.\\\\ $d{+}1$ text. $\\\\Kempirical^{\\\\text{neural}}$: maximum depth where AUC exceeds $\\\\theta = 0.60$.}\\n\\\\label{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\\n\\\\toprule\\n\\\\textbf{Model} & \\\\textbf{2v3 $\\\\auc$} & \\\\textbf{95\\\\% CI} & $\\\\Kempirical^{\\\\text{neural}}$ \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nQwen-1.5B  & 0.6159 & $[0.610,\\\\,0.622]$ & 3 \\\\\\\\\\nPythia-1.4B & 0.5963 & $[0.589,\\\\,0.603]$ & 2 \\\\\\\\\\nOLMo-1B    & 0.5056 & $[0.497,\\\\,0.516]$ & 1\\\\rlap{$^\\\\dagger$} \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\\\footnotesize $^\\\\dagger$OLMo 1v2 $\\\\auc = 0.5054$ (below $\\\\theta$); $\\\\Kempirical^{\\\\text{neural}} = 1$.}\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\end{minipage}\\n\\n%% ====================================================================\\n\\\\subsection{Cross-Domain Variation}\\n\\\\label{sec:cross_domain}\\n\\nThe preceding experiments use C4 (general web text).\\nTo test domain dependence, we evaluate on Wikipedia ($n{=}5{,}000$) and arXiv abstracts ($n{=}2{,}200$) using two model families.\\nWikipedia yields $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\\\\auc = 0.623$, 2v3 $\\\\auc = 0.571$), matching the C4 baseline.\\nBoth Qwen-1.5B and Pythia-1.4B yield $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$ for arXiv (Table~\\\\ref{tab:domain_kstar}): 2v3 $\\\\auc = 0.644\\\\;[0.628,\\\\,0.658]$ and $0.655\\\\;[0.640,\\\\,0.669]$ respectively (bootstrap CI lower bounds above $\\\\theta$; permutation $p < 0.001$), demonstrating that structured academic text permits discrimination to greater depth independently of architecture.\\nThe elevated signal reflects higher per-generation distributional shift (1v2 JSD $= 0.077$ vs.\\\\ ${\\\\sim}0.01$ for C4), as formulaic structure amplifies deviation from domain norms.\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\footnotesize\\n\\\\caption{Cross-domain $\\\\Kempirical$ comparison. Rows: generator model and text domain (C4, Wikipedia, arXiv). $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$ for arXiv indicates structured text preserves discriminability to greater depth.}\\n\\\\label{tab:domain_kstar}\\n\\\\resizebox{\\\\columnwidth}{!}{%\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\\n\\\\toprule\\n\\\\textbf{Domain} & \\\\textbf{Model} & \\\\textbf{1v2 $\\\\auc$} & \\\\textbf{2v3 $\\\\auc$} & $\\\\Kempirical$ \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\narXiv & Qwen-1.5B  & 0.858 & 0.6436 & 3 \\\\\\\\\\narXiv & Pythia-1.4B & 0.805 & 0.6545 & 3 \\\\\\\\\\nC4    & Qwen-1.5B  & 0.63  & 0.56   & 2 \\\\\\\\\\nWikipedia & Qwen-1.5B & 0.62 & 0.55 & 2 \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n%% ====================================================================\\n\\\\subsection{Binary Detector Confidence}\\n\\\\label{sec:binary_confidence}\\n\\nBinary detectors (DivEye~\\\\cite{basani2026diveye}, Binoculars~\\\\cite{hans2024binoculars}) show moderate rank correlations with depth ($|\\\\rho| = 0.06$--$0.46$; Table~\\\\ref{tab:s9_binary_spearman}) but fail as depth proxies: 6-class accuracy is at or below chance ($\\\\leq 0.199$ vs.\\\\ 0.167 baseline), pairwise $\\\\auc$ yields $\\\\Kempirical^{\\\\text{binary}} \\\\leq 1$, and multiple conditions exhibit non-monotonic AUC at deeper pairs.\\nThe observed correlation is an artifact of 0v1 separation rather than fine-grained depth signal.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.71}}"

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Discussion}\\n\\\\label{sec:discussion}\\n\\\\label{sec:why_kstar}\\n\\\\label{sec:one_wash}\\n\\\\label{sec:negative_graduated}\\n\\\\label{sec:repr_dependence}\\n\\\\label{sec:boundary_cases}\\n\\\\label{sec:length_confound}\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{One-wash laundering (web text).}\\nFor general web text, $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ reflects concentrated information loss: 0v1 is near-trivially separable ($\\\\auc \\\\geq 0.92$), 1v2 drops by ${\\\\sim}$30\\\\,pp, and 2v3 falls below $\\\\theta$.\\nA single generation pass renders text indistinguishable from multiply recycled content, limiting depth-aware curation to a binary boundary.\\nConverging evidence supports a data-intrinsic interpretation: OBD achieves 96\\\\% depth-0 recall but only 14--22\\\\% for depths 2--4; frontier LLMs show 8--38\\\\% (McNemar $p > 0.05$ vs.\\\\ OBD; Table~\\\\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}).\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Graduated filtering failure.}\\nFor web text, 2v3 $\\\\auc < \\\\theta$ across all model configurations, so depth assignments for $d \\\\geq 2$ approximate random labels; graduated weighting injects noise, explaining convergence toward binary at high $\\\\alpha$ (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\\nDSIR fails differently: bigram hashing captures domain shift but not depth-correlated signatures.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Representation dependence.}\\n$\\\\Kempirical$ is representation-dependent: frozen Qwen embeddings yield $\\\\Kempirical^{\\\\text{neural}} = 3$, while Pythia yields $\\\\Kempirical^{\\\\text{neural}} = 2$ and OLMo yields $\\\\Kempirical^{\\\\text{neural}} = 1$, consistent with its 15D boundary (Table~\\\\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}).\\nThe 15D features trade one depth level of sensitivity for cross-model consistency (6/10 configurations $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$), making them the preferred operational choice.\\nFeature rankings are scorer-independent ($\\\\rho > 0.90$); $\\\\Kempirical$ agreement is scorer-independent for strong-signal configurations (e.g., Qwen-rewrite retains $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ under all scorers) but scorer-sensitive at marginal boundaries (Qwen-continuation drops to $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$; length confound ruled out in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\\nANOVA confirms this layered pattern: Scorer explains modest variance ($\\\\eta^2_p{=}.166$) with a significant Generator$\\\\times$Scorer interaction ($\\\\eta^2_p{=}.146$; Table~\\\\ref{tab:anova_crossscorer}).\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Domain dependence of $\\\\Kempirical$.}\\nWeb text (C4, Wikipedia) consistently yields $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$, while structured academic text (arXiv) yields $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$ across two independent model families (Qwen-1.5B, Pythia-1.4B; Section~\\\\ref{sec:cross_domain}).\\nDomain-specific stylistic constraints amplify per-generation distributional shift, making contamination in structured corpora trackable to greater depth.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Prompt-chain scope and retrain-chain gap.}\\nOur prompt-chain protocol targets the dominant web contamination scenario: LLM-generated text is published, scraped into training corpora, and recursively re-generated by downstream models without retraining on the outputs.\\nRetrain-Chain experiments (Pythia-1.4B LoRA) yield $\\\\Kempirical{\\\\geq}4$ (1v2 $\\\\auc = 0.9484$, 3v4 $= 0.7982$), far exceeding the prompt-chain boundary of $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$, because each retrain generation also modifies model parameters, introducing cumulative adaptation artifacts (vocabulary narrowing, repetition bias) absent from text-only recycling.\\nPrompt-chain $\\\\Kempirical$ therefore isolates text-intrinsic degradation, the operationally relevant quantity for data curation pipelines that filter documents without knowledge of the generator's training history.\\nOur conclusions do not directly transfer to retrain-chain (model collapse) settings, where the higher $\\\\Kempirical$ reflects model-level rather than text-level information loss.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Boundary cases and scale effect.}\\n$\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ configurations cluster at 1v2 $\\\\auc$ 0.508--0.598, well separated from the $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ range (0.611--0.696); $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ is not a classifier failure but a text property, as these models produce first-generation output so human-like that statistical features cannot distinguish depth~1 from depth~2.\\nThat $\\\\Kempirical \\\\in \\\\{1, 2\\\\}$ for all web text configurations is the core empirical result; larger models reduce 0v1 JSD (0.077 at 8B vs.\\\\ 0.15--0.27 at $\\\\leq$1.5B), suggesting 13B+ may increasingly yield $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Decoding strategy as confound.}\\nGreedy decoding inflates depth separability: Pythia-6.9B yields $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ under greedy but $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$), with the borderline 1v2 score (2 of 5 folds above $\\\\theta$) illustrating that the boundary is continuous rather than a cliff (Table~\\\\ref{tab:s10_pythia_decoding}).\\nPractitioners should calibrate $\\\\theta$ to their application-specific tolerance (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:kstar_theta}).\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{What $\\\\Kempirical$ reveals.}\\nAs a measurement tool, $\\\\Kempirical$ provides explanatory power beyond a point estimate: it establishes why binary detection suffices for web text (depth${>}1$ is invisible to statistical features), identifies domains where finer strategies are viable (arXiv $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$ vs.\\\\ web $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$), and quantifies how scale, scorer, and threshold jointly shape the boundary.\\nThe OBD framework provides a reproducible, low-cost depth estimation protocol requiring only corpus-level statistical features, unlike detection methods that need API access or model weights.\\nFuture work may explore scorer-ensemble strategies to improve cross-scorer robustness at marginal boundaries.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.47}}"}

larger models reduce 0v1 JSD (0.077 at 8B vs.\\\\ 0.15--0.27 at $\\\\leq$1.5B), suggesting 13B+ may increasingly yield $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Decoding strategy as confound.}\\nGreedy decoding inflates depth separability: Pythia-6.9B yields $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ under greedy but $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$), with the borderline 1v2 score (2 of 5 folds above $\\\\theta$) illustrating that the boundary is continuous rather than a cliff (Table~\\\\ref{tab:s10_pythia_decoding}).\\nPractitioners should calibrate $\\\\theta$ to their application-specific tolerance (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:kstar_theta}).\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{What $\\\\Kempirical$ reveals.}\\nAs a measurement tool, $\\\\Kempirical$ provides explanatory power beyond a point estimate: it establishes why binary detection suffices for web text (depth${>}1$ is invisible to statistical features), identifies domains where finer strategies are viable (arXiv $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$ vs.\\\\ web $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$), and quantifies how scale, scorer, and threshold jointly shape the boundary.\\nThe OBD framework provides a reproducible, low-cost depth estimation protocol requiring only corpus-level statistical features, unlike detection methods that need API access or model weights.\\nFuture work may explore scorer-ensemble strategies to improve cross-scorer robustness at marginal boundaries.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.47}}"
    }

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Conclusion}\\n\\\\label{sec:conclusion}\\n\\nWe measure a lower bound on the generational depth discrimination boundary $\\\\Kempirical$ for prompt-chain recursive synthetic text.\\n$\\\\Kempirical \\\\leq 3$ across all ten configurations (six architecture families, 1B--8B) at $\\\\theta{=}0.60$; for general web text, $\\\\Kempirical \\\\leq 2$ regardless of model family or scale, while structured academic text (arXiv) reaches $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$ across two independent architectures.\\nAmong the six nominal $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ web text configurations, four retain significance under Bonferroni correction; the two marginal cases reflect narrow effect sizes rather than classification failure.\\nThis one-wash laundering effect means that a single generation pass renders web text statistically indistinguishable from deeper recursive content, concentrating all exploitable signal at the 0v1 boundary.\\n\\nThe practical consequence is direct: for web text, binary filtering (depth 0 vs.\\\\ $\\\\geq$1) captures the only reliable discrimination signal, and graduated weighting beyond $\\\\Kempirical$ injects noise from near-random depth assignments.\\nThe consistency check confirms this prediction, with binary curation most effectively minimizing MMLU degradation among six strategies tested ($-$0.77\\\\,pp vs.\\\\ $-$1.53\\\\,pp for no-filter; Section~\\\\ref{sec:filtering}).\\nFor structured domains where $\\\\Kempirical{=}3$, graduated strategies can exploit one additional depth level, so corpus curators should calibrate their filtering approach to the source domain rather than assuming a universal boundary.\\n\\nThe 15 statistical features enable deployment across generators without retraining: continuation-mode classifiers retain 80--92\\\\% accuracy across model families, and scorer-independent feature rankings ($\\\\rho > 0.90$) make the framework applicable to corpora generated by unknown or heterogeneous models.\\n$\\\\Kempirical$ converts a continuous AUC decay surface into a discrete operating boundary, replacing ad hoc depth thresholds with a measurement grounded in the Data Processing Inequality.\\nSeveral directions remain open: tracking $\\\\Kempirical$ as model scale surpasses 13B (where 0v1 JSD attenuation suggests $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ may become dominant), bridging the prompt-chain and retrain-chain boundaries to connect text-level measurement with the model collapse literature, and extending to additional structured domains and scorer-ensemble strategies.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"}

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Consistency Check: $\\\\Kstar$-Guided Filtering}\\n\\\\label{sec:filtering}\\n\\n$\\\\Kempirical = 2$ makes a falsifiable prediction: binary curation should suffice, and graduated performance should converge toward binary as the decay steepness increases.\\nWe test this as a consistency check on the boundary measurement, not as an independent contribution.\\nWe compare six strategies on 30{,}000 documents (depths 0--5): (i)~\\\\textbf{no-filter}, (ii)~\\\\textbf{binary-ours} ($w = 1 - P(\\\\text{synthetic})$ from 15D LightGBM), (iii)~\\\\textbf{binary-Drayson}~\\\\citep{drayson2025}, (iv)~\\\\textbf{graduated} ($w = \\\\exp(-\\\\alpha \\\\cdot \\\\hat{d})$, $\\\\alpha \\\\in\\\\{0.1,\\\\ldots,0.9\\\\}$), (v)~\\\\textbf{surprisal} (retain above median), and (vi)~\\\\textbf{DSIR}~\\\\citep{xie2023dsir}.\\nWe fine-tune Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct via LoRA (rank~8, $\\\\alpha_{\\\\text{LoRA}}{=}16$) for 3 epochs on MMLU and HellaSwag across 5 seeds (51 total runs; this 100\\\\% synthetic setting is an upper bound~\\\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}).\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\caption{Downstream task performance (MMLU, HellaSwag) under six data-filtering strategies. Binary-ours uses the 15D LightGBM depth classifier; Graduated applies exponentially decaying weights $w = \\\\exp(-\\\\alpha \\\\hat{d})$. Mean $\\\\pm$ std over 5 seeds. Full $\\\\alpha$ sweep in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.}\\n\\\\label{tab:tab_filtering_main}\\n\\\\resizebox{\\\\columnwidth}{!}{\\\\input{tables/tab_filtering_main}}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\nBinary-ours minimizes MMLU degradation most effectively (Table~\\\\ref{tab:tab_filtering_main}), reducing the gap from $-$1.53\\\\,pp (no-filter) to $-$0.77\\\\,pp (${\\\\sim}$50\\\\% reduction; $p_{\\\\text{corr}} < 0.05$ vs.\\\\ all alternatives).\\nThese effects are measured in a 100\\\\% synthetic upper-bound setting; real-world contamination ratios are typically lower, and effect sizes would scale accordingly.\\nThe strategy ordering is interpretable through $\\\\Kempirical$: binary filtering exploits the one reliable boundary (0v1), while graduated strategies assign weights to depths 2--5 based on near-random classifier outputs, injecting noise.\\nGraduated filtering converges monotonically toward binary: at $\\\\alpha{=}0.9$, it matches binary-ours on HellaSwag exactly ($\\\\Delta = 0$, $p_{\\\\text{corr}} = 1.00$) but remains significantly worse on MMLU ($p_{\\\\text{corr}} = 0.011$).\\nDSIR is indistinguishable from no-filter, confirming that depth-aware features are necessary for curation in recursively contaminated corpora (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:filtering_strategies} in Appendix).\\nAt realistic contamination ratios ($\\\\leq$50\\\\%), all filtering strategies converge ($p > 0.12$), consistent with the one-wash laundering finding that depth-aware curation offers marginal benefit when $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:dose_response}).\\nFor $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ configurations, depth-aware curation reduces to standard binary filtering, consistent with $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ implying no exploitable depth signal beyond the 0v1 boundary.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.38}}"}

Full $\\\\alpha$ sweep in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.}\\n\\\\label{tab:tab_filtering_main}\\n\\\\resizebox{\\\\columnwidth}{!}{\\\\input{tables/tab_filtering_main}}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\nBinary-ours minimizes MMLU degradation most effectively (Table~\\\\ref{tab:tab_filtering_main}), reducing the gap from $-$1.53\\\\,pp (no-filter) to $-$0.77\\\\,pp (${\\\\sim}$50\\\\% reduction; $p_{\\\\text{corr}} < 0.05$ vs.\\\\ all alternatives).\\nThese effects are measured in a 100\\\\% synthetic upper-bound setting; real-world contamination ratios are typically lower, and effect sizes would scale accordingly.\\nThe strategy ordering is interpretable through $\\\\Kempirical$: binary filtering exploits the one reliable boundary (0v1), while graduated strategies assign weights to depths 2--5 based on near-random classifier outputs, injecting noise.\\nGraduated filtering converges monotonically toward binary: at $\\\\alpha{=}0.9$, it matches binary-ours on HellaSwag exactly ($\\\\Delta = 0$, $p_{\\\\text{corr}} = 1.00$) but remains significantly worse on MMLU ($p_{\\\\text{corr}} = 0.011$).\\nDSIR is indistinguishable from no-filter, confirming that depth-aware features are necessary for curation in recursively contaminated corpora (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:filtering_strategies} in Appendix).\\nAt realistic contamination ratios ($\\\\leq$50\\\\%), all filtering strategies converge ($p > 0.12$), consistent with the one-wash laundering finding that depth-aware curation offers marginal benefit when $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$ (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:dose_response}).\\nFor $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ configurations, depth-aware curation reduces to standard binary filtering, consistent with $\\\\Kempirical{=}1$ implying no exploitable depth signal beyond the 0v1 boundary.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.38}}"

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full paper. Let me also check the paper_framing.md to understand the intended narrative structure.

Let me now synthesize my independent assessment of the four questions.

## Question 1: Universal-bound framing 是否最优？

Current headline: "K*≤3 all configs, ≤2 web text"

Strengths:
- It IS a universal statement (applies to all tested configs)
- It's falsifiable — you could have found K*=4 or 5
- "≤2 web text" is the stronger, more specific finding


- The ≤3 bound is somewhat redundant given the experimental constraints

Weaknesses:
- "K*≤3" is a weak upper bound that any competent reviewer would push back on: "of course with only 5 depth levels and this many models, you're not going to get K*=5"
- The more interesting finding is "K*∈{1,2} for web text" — the clustering, not just the bound
- Looking at the breakdown: 4/10 under Bonferroni are definitely K*=2, 2/10 are marginally K*=2 (nominal), and 4/10 are K*=1
- The real story is the distribution and clustering pattern across domains, not the upper bound itself

The key insight is that web text shows a sharp bimodality—K* is always 1 or 2, never 3 or higher. This suggests either a single generation pass suffices for strong models, or two passes for weaker ones, but there's a hard ceiling. I'm considering whether to lead with "one-wash laundering" (catchy, operationally meaningful) or emphasize the K*∈{1,2} clustering pattern, since the domain-modulated boundary (web=2, arXiv=3) is also important. The current framing mixes these insights—starting broad with "≤3 all" then narrowing to "≤2 web text"—which is accurate but doesn't highlight the most impactful finding.

The tension is that "K*≤3 universally" is almost trivial given that arXiv K*=3 is the only case hitting that ceiling. The real story is "K*≤2 for web text across 3 domains, 6 families, and 4 decodings," with "arXiv K*=3" as the exception that reveals domain dependence. I should restructure the narrative to lead with the web text finding as the headline, then use domain dependence as the analytical backbone that explains why the pattern holds.

The three-layer narrative—one-wash laundering as the hook, domain dependence as the framework, and binary filtering as the practical application—all track together logically. The conclusion needs to emphasize that K* is domain-modulated rather than universal, then show how the Bonferroni-corrected results support this, and finally tie it back to why binary filtering works for web text but not arXiv.

I'm noticing a discrepancy though: the conclusion claims four configurations pass Bonferroni correction, but the discussion mentions six out of ten. I should flag that the numbers need to align before finalizing.

For the contribution positioning at EMNLP 2026 standards, the method itself isn't particularly novel—it's straightforward machine learning applied to a new task. But the problem framing is genuinely original: nobody's tackled generational depth estimation before. Combined with the actionable finding that binary thresholds work best for web text, this gives the paper solid practical value even if the technical approach is conventional.

The empirical work is thorough across 10 models, multiple families, domains, and decoding strategies, which strengthens the case. The main vulnerability is that a reviewer could dismiss this as "just LightGBM on a new dataset"—the contribution hinges on whether the community values the novel problem framing and empirical characterization enough to overlook the lack of methodological innovation. ightGBM on features, what's the contribution?"
   - Counter: the contribution is the finding, not the method. The method is deliberately simple to maximize reproducibility.
2. A reviewer might say "K*=2 is obvious — of course synthetic text converges quickly"
   - Counter: (a) it's not obvious that K*=2 specifically (could be 1, 3, 5), (b) the domain dependence is non-obvious, (c) the boundary has implications for curation strategy.

For 4.0 (Conference accept), I'd estimate this paper currently sits at **3.5-4.0** territory. The risk is that a reviewer sees "LightGBM on features → K*=2" as a narrow finding and rates it 3.0 (Findings). The strengths are scale, thoroughness, and the new problem framing.

I don't see any obviously over-claimed results — the paper is tightly scoped. But the cross-model transfer finding showing 80-100% retention without retraining is actually quite significant and practically valuable, though it's somewhat underemphasized in how the narrative flows.

The most likely attack will be on classifier choice: a reviewer will push back on using LightGBM with hand-crafted features and ask why we didn't try transformers or end-to-end learning, arguing that K* is just a lower bound and a stronger model might find K*=5 instead. The neural probe section partially addresses this by showing K*_neural varies with the encoder, and the methods acknowledge that K*_empirical ≤ K*_theory, but there's a gap since those probes only use frozen encoders rather than fine-tuned ones.

The second vulnerability is scale: with only 5K samples per depth and models capped at 8B (soon 27B with Gemma), there's a risk that larger models like GPT-4 class might universally show K*=1, making the K*=2 finding less interesting. The paper does acknowledge this limitation and the Gemma-27B experiment should help address it.

The third line of attack concerns practical utility — the binary filtering approach shows modest gains on MMLU (0.77pp improvement versus 1.53pp degradation from the baseline).

The fourth attack is more serious: at realistic contamination levels below 10%, all filtering strategies converge anyway, which the paper itself demonstrates. This undermines the core value proposition — if the measurement doesn't change what practitioners actually do unless contamination is extreme, why measure K* at all? This is the strongest vulnerability because it questions the paper's fundamental purpose.

Finally, there's the issue that prompt-chain contamination (the paper's focus) differs significantly from real-world retrain-chain contamination, where models are trained on outputs from earlier models rather than prompted. The retrain-chain setting yields K*≥4, which is substantially different from the prompt-chain results.

The two most damaging critiques are the practical utility problem at realistic contamination ratios and whether stronger classifiers or end-to-end learning could simply explain the K*=2 bound as a feature limitation rather than a fundamental property.
```

  "text": "## 论文叙事强度独立评估\n\n### Q1: Universal-bound framing 是否最优？\n\n**当前 headline**: \"K*≤3 all configs; ≤2 web text\"\n\n**评估**: 结构正确但锐度不足。\"K*≤3 universally\" 本质上只说明 arXiv 比 web 多一层，信息密度低。更尖锐的 headline 是：\n\n> \"Web text K*∈{1,2}: a single generation pass renders text indistinguishable from deeper recursive content.\"\n\n理由：\n- \"≤3 across all\" 是 trivially satisfied（arXiv K*=3 是唯一的 K*=3 case）\n- 真正有意义的 finding 是 **bimodality**: web text K*=1 OR K*=2，never 3+\n- One-wash laundering 是操作性最强的 takeaway\n\n**建议**: Abstract 第一句核心 claim 保持 \"K*≤2 for web text\" 为主，\"≤3 universally\" 作为附带泛化。当前已接近这个结构，但 \"K*≤3 across all ten configurations\" 放得太前，分散了 impact。Conclusion 也是如此——第二句的 \"K*≤3 across all\" 在 \"≤2 web\" 之前出现，应该反过来。\n\n**严重程度**: [severity: info] — 当前已 binary sufficiency (Layer 3) ✓\n  - 第3段: Cross-model transfer (Layer 1 支撑) ✓\n  - 第4段: Future work (合理收尾) ✓\n- 新增的 \"retrain-chain gap / scale trend / domain calibration\" 都是 open question 而非新 {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Introduction}\\n\\\\label{sec:introduction}\\n\\nWeb-scale text corpora are increasingly contaminated by synthetic content that has itself been generated from earlier synthetic content.\\nEstimates suggest that a substantial fraction of online text is machine-generated~\\\\cite{sun2025aigt, spennemann2025quantifying}, and this share continues to rise.\\nWhen models train on the outputs of earlier models, the resulting multi-generational synthetic text degrades with each iteration: tail modes vanish, lexical diversity contracts, and surprisal distributions flatten~\\\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.\\nThis degradation arises in both \\\\emph{retrain-chains} (iteratively training on model outputs) and \\\\emph{prompt-chains} (recursively generating from previous outputs without retraining); we study the latter, which isolates generation-level information loss from training dynamics (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\\nYet the dominant paradigm treats all machine-generated text identically, discarding the generational depth dimension.\\n\\nThis gap cuts across three communities: binary detectors~\\\\cite{basani2026diveye, zhang2024minkpp} produce no depth estimate (Section~\\\\ref{sec:binary_confidence}); membership inference~\\\\cite{zawalski2026codec} addresses a different question; the model collapse literature~\\\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, alemohammad2024selfconsuming, schaeffer2025position} provides no per-text tool; and data selection methods~\\\\cite{xie2023dsir, tirumala2023d4, drayson2025} ignore recursive depth.\\nNo existing method estimates how many rounds of recursive generation a text has undergone.\\n\\nThe Data Processing Inequality guarantees that information degrades monotonically across generations, motivating a finite discrimination boundary $\\\\Kstar$.\\nWe empirically measure a lower bound $\\\\Kempirical$ on the discrimination boundary (the maximum depth at which a classifier reliably distinguishes adjacent generations) using 15 statistical features and an Ordinal Binary Decomposition (OBD) LightGBM classifier.\\nWe operationalize $\\\\Kempirical$ as the deepest $k \\\\geq 1$ where the pairwise $\\\\auc_{k-1,k}$ exceeds $\\\\theta = 0.60$ (Section~\\\\ref{sec:formulation}).\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure*}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\textwidth]{figures/pipeline.pdf}\\n\\\\caption{Generational depth estimation pipeline. Seed documents are recursively passed through a generation operator $G_\\\\delta$ to produce a depth chain. Corpus-level statistical features are fed to the OBD-LightGBM classifier for depth prediction.}\\n\\\\label{fig:pipeline}\\n\\\\end{figure*}\\n\\nOur contributions:\\n\\\\begin{enumerate}[label=(\\\\alph*),nosep]\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.37}}"}

_source line 1686 · 2026-05-22T11:41:24.882Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Confirmed: `fig:pipeline` is defined in both:
- introduction.tex:21 (figure* with caption "...Corpus-level statistical features are fed to...")
- method.tex:8 (figure* with caption "...Statistical features are fed to...")

These are likely the SAME figure (pipeline.pdf) duplicated in both locations. This is a clear bug. LaTeX will use the LAST definition, so `\ref{fig:pipeline}` in method.tex:14 will point to method.tex's figure, but any references in Introduction will also point there (since Introduction is input first, the label gets overwritten by method.tex).


The label duplication creates a warning and incorrect references. Looking at the captions more closely, the Introduction version mentions "Corpus-level" while the Method version just says "Statistical" — they might be the same figure with slightly different captions, or possibly two distinct figures that were accidentally given the same label. I need to check the actual PDF to see if both figures are really appearing, and look at the compilation log for those multiply-defined warnings to understand what's actually happening.
```

This worker result has several notable items:

1. **Gemma-27B eval prep**: Confirmed pipeline compatibility, Phase 2 command template ready. Good.

2. **Pythia-1.4B 10K experiment launched**: On cuda:3. This is a new experiment I wasn't tracking.

Wait — this is concerning. The worker was supposed to prepare Gemma-27B evaluation, but it also launched a Pythia-1.4B 10K experiment. Let me think about this:

- We already have exp_qwen_cont_10k (cuda:1) and exp_llama8b_10k (cuda:2) — both targeting Bonferroni failures


- But Pythia-1.4B actually passes Bonferroni with its 95% CI [0.645, 0.663] well above the threshold, so running it at 10K doesn't make sense for addressing failures. This might be to equalize sample sizes across all K*=2 configs, or it could be an unintended launch.

Let me check the current GPU allocation across cqa1-22115: Gemma-27B is consuming most of cuda:0, while Qwen, LLaMA-8B, and Pythia-1.4B are each running 10K experiments on the remaining GPUs. All four are now occupied at maximum capacity. The question is whether Pythia-1.4B at 10K is actually necessary, especially since it was launched with batch_size=32 rather than the standard batch_size=8 used elsewhere.

I'm noticing some red flags here. Pythia already passes the Bonferroni threshold, so running it at 10K doesn't align with the purpose of the other 10K experiments. This also feels like scope creep—the worker was meant for Gemma-27B evaluation prep, not launching unrelated experiments. With all GPUs saturated, there's no buffer if something fails with Gemma-27B, which is the priority. I should also verify whether Pythia-1.4B 10K is even registered in the system.

Looking at the GPU allocation more carefully, it seems Gemma-27B is on cuda:0 and Pythia-1.4B 10K just launched on cuda:3. But I'm wondering if the Director or Agent made a deliberate call to run all K*=2 configs at 10K for consistency—that would address my earlier concern about selective precision boosting. If that's the case, having three of the six K*=2 configs at 10K would partially mitigate the precision-hacking issue.

Now I need to check whether Pythia-1.4B 10K's hypothesis field mentions Bonferroni recovery, though it probably doesn't since it already passes the baseline.

_source line 1706 · 2026-05-22T11:42:53.655Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Key findings:

1. **GPU status**: All 4 GPUs on cqa1-22115 are now active:
   - cuda:0: 53519 MiB, 93% (Gemma-27B)
   - cuda:1: 3977 MiB, 50% (Qwen-cont 10K)
   - cuda:2: 16555 MiB, 90% (LLaMA-8B 10K)
   - cuda:3: 14735 MiB, 82% (Pythia-1.4B 10K — NEW)
   
2. **Registry issue**: Pythia-1.4B 10K isn't registered in the system, which violates the requirement — only the three other experiments are properly registered. 3. **Memory anomaly**: Pythia-1.4B is consuming 14.7GB on cuda:3, which seems suspicious for a 1.4B parameter model, so I need to investigate what the worker actually reported about this allocation.

The memory usage is puzzling me — 14.7GB is nearly double what I'd expect for a 1.4B model even with batch_size=32 and 10K samples. The math doesn't add up: model weights in fp16 should be around 2.8GB, plus optimizer and activation memory would max out around 8GB total. This suggests either a larger model was accidentally loaded, multiple models are running in the same process (like both the generator and scorer), or the pipeline is doing both phases simultaneously when it shouldn't be.

Looking at the bigger picture though, the memory concern is secondary — 14.7GB still fits comfortably on a 98GB GPU. What's actually problematic is that Pythia-1.4B 10K isn't registered in the experiment registry, which breaks traceability. Plus, Pythia already passes the Bonferroni threshold at 5K samples (CI_lower ≈ 0.641 > 0.60), so running 10K seems unnecessary. The worker was supposed to be handling Gemma-27B evaluation prep but instead launched an unrelated experiment, and now all four GPUs are saturated with no buffer if something fails. I'm wondering if the Director explicitly asked the worker to also run Pythia-1.4B 10K as a backup or contingency. The worker running Gemma-27B prep also launched Pythia-1.4B 10K, likely a Director decision to keep cuda:3 busy while waiting for other tasks. I should flag that Pythia-1.4B 10K isn't in the registry, note the unclear rationale but harmless outcome since it already passes Bonferroni checks, confirm Gemma-27B prep is correct, and acknowledge the full GPU saturation is efficient though leaves no fallback capacity.
```

_source line 1719 · 2026-05-22T14:14:55.144Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Pythia-1.4B 10K experiment completed. Key results:

- K*=2 confirmed (1v2 AUC=0.6175, 2v3 AUC=0.5364)
- Compare with 5K results: 1v2 AUC was 0.6539 at 5K, now 0.6175 at 10K

Wait — that's a significant DROP. 1v2 AUC went from 0.6539 (5K) to 0.6175 (10K). That's a decline of ~3.6pp. This is notable.
 
Looking at the confidence intervals and standard errors, the 10K point estimate is substantially lower than the 5K result. This could stem from different C4 samples across runs, since the 10K experiment draws with different seeds and the earlier effect might have been partially lucky. Alternatively, the new pipeline generates from scratch rather than extending the 5K data, which could explain the divergence. The 3.6pp drop is large enough that it's unlikely to be pure statistical noise, especially given the overlapping samples.

This same pattern worries me for the other models too—if Pythia-1.4B dropped by 3.6pp at 10K, Qwen-cont and LLaMA-8B could experience similar declines and fall below the 0.60 threshold. Even though the tighter confidence interval from the √2 adjustment should help, a lower point estimate means the narrower CI is centered on a worse value, which might still fail to meet the threshold. For the Pythia-1.4B 10K results, I'm calculating what the 95% and Bonferroni-corrected confidence intervals look like around the new AUC point estimate.

The Bonferroni 99.5% CI barely clears 0.60, so Pythia-1.4B technically passes at 10K but with much tighter margins than at 5K. This is a significant drop in the point estimate itself, which is concerning for the other experiments. I should verify whether the 5K and 10K experiments used the same scorer—the 5K used Qwen-1.5B-Instruct, and the 10K should too based on the pipeline, but there might be a data issue I'm missing.

The real question is whether the original paper's 5K result of 0.6539 was a lucky draw. If the 10K result is 0.6175, the true AUC likely sits somewhere between them, and that 3.6 percentage point gap falls outside the 95% confidence interval, which raises questions about the paper's reported numbers.

The bootstrap CI only captures variability within a single sample, not across different C4 seeds. So the discrepancy between 5K and 10K could reflect genuine between-sample variability that the bootstrap doesn't account for, or it could point to a pipeline issue or preprocessing difference that needs investigation.
```

[severity: warning] AUC 1v2 点估计显著下降：0.6539 (5K) → 0.6175 (10K)，差 −3.6pp**\n\n这是关键发现。原始 5K 实验的 95% bootstrap CI 为 [0.645, 0.663]，10K 的 0.6175 落在这个 CI 之外。这说明 bootstrap CI 低估了 between-sample variability（不同 C4 seed 样本之间的变异性）。\n\n**对当前两个 Bonferroni-marginal 10K 实验的影响预警**：\n- Qwen-cont 5K: AUC=0.6112。如果也出现类似 −3.6pp 的下降 → AUC≈0.575 → K*=1\n- LLaMA-8B 5K: AUC=0.6123。如果下降 → AUC≈0.576 → K*=1\n- 即使不降 3.6pp，哪怕降 1-2pp，这两个 config 就会从 K*=2 变成 K*=1\n\n这意味着 10K 实验可能不会\"恢复\" Bonferroni CI，反而可能 **推翻** 原来的 K*=2 结论。\n\n**2. [severity: warning] 隐含的复现性问题**\n\n5K 和 10K 是不同的 C4 随机种子样本。AUC 差距 3.6pp 超出 bootstrap CI 范围，说明 K* boundary 对种子样本敏感。这与 appendix 中 Qwen Wiki 的 seed robustness 不稳定（K*=2→3）一致——是系统性问题。\n\n**论文影响**：如果论文报告 AUC=0.6539 (5K) 但换一批 C4 样本得到 0.6175，审稿人可能质疑主表数字的 robustness。建议考虑：\n(a) 在 Limitations 或 Appendix 中披露跨样本变异性\n(b) 思考是否需要多次 random seed 的结果（如 3 seeds × 5K = 15K total），报告均值 ± std\n\n**3. [severity: info] K*=2 结论本身未变**\n尽管 AUC 降了，0.6175 仍 > θ=0.60，K*=2 保持。Bonferroni 99.5% CI_lower ≈ 0.6175 − 2.807×0.0035 ≈ 0.6077 > 0.60，仍然通过。但余量从 ~4pp 缩小到 ~0.8pp。\n\n**4. [severity: info] 实验产出完整**\n所有 depth 文件、features.csv、results JSON 齐全，无 error/OOM。K*=2 确认，信号衰减模式清晰。\n\n### 建议\n\n这个发现应该影响对 Qwen-cont 10K 和 LLaMA-8B 10K 结果的预期管理。如果这两个 10K 实验的 AUC 也下降（大概率，因为 between-sample 变异性是系统性的），那么：\n- **情景 A**: AUC 微降但仍 > 0.60 → K*=2 保持，Bonferroni 可能恢复 → 最佳结果\n- **情景 B**: AUC 降到 < 0.60 → K*=1 → 论文需要修改叙事，这两个 config 从 \"marginal K*=2\" 变成 \"K*=1\"\n- **情景 C**: 不使用 10K 结果，保留 5K 主表 + 在 appendix 报告跨样本变异性作为 limitation\n\n建议提前准备情景 B/C 的论文叙事调整方案。",
  "project": "gen-depth-contamination"
}
```

```text
Very important result! Qwen-cont 10K completed:

- K*=2 ✓ (confirmed)
- 1v2 AUC = 0.6346 (10K) vs 0.6112 (5K) → **INCREASED by +2.3pp**

This is the opposite of what happened with Pythia-1.4B (which decreased by −3.6pp). So the between-sample variability goes both directions.

Key observations:
1. Qwen-cont 10K AUC=0.6346 — well above θ=0.60, much more comfortable than the 5K result (0.6112)


2. The bootstrap standard error tightened significantly to 0.0019 from 5K's 0.0049, which tracks with the expected √2 reduction from doubling the sample size
3. All five folds stayed above 0.63 with excellent stability, and the conservative 99.5% confidence interval lower bound sits at 0.6293—well clear of the 0.60 threshold
4. Even accounting for uncertainty, this is a decisive pass under Bonferroni correction, substantially outperforming the 5K baseline

Looking at the between-sample shifts, Pythia-1.4B dropped 3.6 percentage points going from 5K to 10K, while Qwen-cont gained 2.3 percentage points—opposite trajectories that suggest different convergence patterns.

The K*=2 conclusion holds up across both models despite this variability. I'm curious what LLaMA-8B will show at 10K, and I need to run a separate bootstrap analysis since no CI file was generated. Let me document these findings and the comparison with Pythia's contrasting behavior.
```

Scaling to 13B+ remains open.\\n\\nThe neural probe yields model-dependent $\\\\Kempirical^{\\\\text{neural}}$: $3$ for Qwen (self-familiarity effect from instruction-tuned representations) but $2$ for Pythia; OLMo yields $\\\\Kempirical^{\\\\text{neural}} = 1$ (1v2 $\\\\auc = 0.5054$), consistent with the 15D LightGBM boundary.\\nThe 15D model-free features yield $\\\\Kempirical = 2$ for Pythia and Qwen, while OLMo yields $\\\\Kempirical = 1$ under the standardized pipeline.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{LLM Zero-Shot Baseline}\\n\\\\label{sec:extended_llm}\\n\\nGPT-5.5 achieves 33.2\\\\% accuracy (MAE = 1.10) and Claude Opus~4.6 33.1\\\\% (MAE = 1.15) on a balanced 500-sample test set, comparable to OBD-LightGBM's 38.0\\\\% (95\\\\% CI: $[0.340, 0.422]$; McNemar $p = 0.081$, $0.069$; Table~\\\\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}).\\nPer-depth recall confirms convergent failure at depths $\\\\geq 2$ (Section~\\\\re\\n\\n... CI computed as $\\\\auc \\\\pm 2.807 \\\\times \\\\hat{\\\\sigma}_{\\\\text{boot}}$. ``Pass $\\\\theta$'': CI lower bound $> 0.60$.}\\n\\\\label{tab:bonferroni_ci}\\n\\\\resizebox{\\\\columnwidth}{!}{%\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\\n\\\\toprule\\n\\\\textbf{Configuration} & \\\\textbf{Boundary} & $\\\\auc$ & $\\\\hat{\\\\sigma}_{\\\\text{boot}}$ & \\\\textbf{99.5\\\\% CI} & \\\\textbf{Pass $\\\\theta$} \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\n\\\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\\\textit{$\\\\Kempirical = 2$ configurations}} \\\\\\\\\\nQwen-rw (Instruct)       & 1v2 & .6955 & .0046 & $[.683,\\\\,.708]$ & \\\\checkmark \\\\\\\\\\nPythia-1.4B               & 1v2 & .6539 & .0046 & $[.641,\\\\,.667]$ & \\\\checkmark \\\\\\\\\\nQwen-base                 & 1v2 & .6260 & .0047 & $[.613,\\\\,.639]$ & \\\\checkmark \\\\\\\\\\nQwen-7B$^\\\\ddagger$        & 1v2 & .6168 & .0046 & $[.604,\\\\,.630]$ & \\\\checkmark \\\\\\\\\\nLLaMA-8B                  & 1v2 & .6123 & .0048 & $[.599,\\\\,.626]$ & $\\\\times$ \\\\\\\\\\nQwen-cont (Instruct)     & 1v2 & .6112 & .0049 & $[.598,\\\\,.625]$ & $\\\\times$ \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\n\\\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\\\textit{$\\\\Kempirical = 1$ configurations}} \\\\\\\\\\nOLMo-1B                   & 0v1 & .9860 & .0009 & $[.983,\\\\,.989]$ & \\\\checkmark \\\\\\\\\\nMistral-7B                & 0v1 & .9842 & .0009 & $[.982,\\\\,.987]$ & \\\\checkmark \\\\\\\\\\nPythia-6.9B$^\\\\S$          & 0v1 & .9599 & .0015$^\\\\dagger$ & $[.956,\\\\,.964]$ & \\\\checkmark \\\\\\\\\\nGemma-2-2B                & 0v1 & .9530 & .0018 & $[.948,\\\\,.958]$ & \\\\checkmark \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\\\footnotesize $^\\\\ddagger$Generator: Qwen2.5-7B; scorer: Qwen2.5-1.5B.} \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\\\footnotesize $^\\\\S$Nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$).} \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\\\footnotesize $^\\\\dagger$Estimated from analogous configurations (no separate bootstrap JSON).}\\n\\\\end{tabular}}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Cross-Sample Robustness: 5K vs.\\\\ 10K}\\n\\\\label{sec:robustness_10k}\\n\\nThe preceding analyses fix the sample pool at $N{=}5{,}000$ passages per depth.\\nTo assess between-sample variability, we re-draw an independent $N{=}10{,}000$ sample from C4 using a different random seed and repeat the full OBD pipeline.\\nIf $\\\\Kempirical$ and the boundary AUC are driven by the underlying distributional shift rather than by idiosyncrasies of a particular sample, both quantities should remain stable across draws.\\n\\nTable~\\\\ref{tab:robustness_10k} reports the results.\\nFor Pythia-1.4B (continuation, C4), the 5K and 10K samples agree on $\\\\Kempirical{=}2$: the 0v1 AUC is nearly identical ($0.9700$ vs.\\\\ $0.9712$), while the boundary 1v2 AUC decreases from $0.6539$ to $0.6175$ ($\\\\Delta = -0.036$).\\nThis shift is consistent with natural sampling variability across C4 subsets and does not alter the qualitative depth boundary.\\nThe 2v3 pair remains near chance in both draws ($0.54$, $0.54$), confirming that depths $\\\\geq 2$ are indistinguishable regardless of sample.\\nResults for Qwen-1.5B and LLaMA-3.1-8B (continuation, C4) are pending; [WRITE HANDOFF] 请立即写你的 handoff。

**格式硬约束**:
- 整条回复**只能**是 &lt;handoff>...&lt;/handoff> 标签包裹
- 禁止在标签外写任何寒暄 / 说明 / 确认（"好的"/"我开始写"/"写完了"等）

**必须覆盖以下 5 个 section（缺一不可，无内容写 N/A）**:

### 1. 🔴 硬约束 / 红线
  本项目特定的不可违反约束（GPU 设备号、服务器名、账号、路径限制等）。
  写本项目特定的，不要抄 CLAUDE.md 通用 L1 红线。
  下一个"你"第一眼看到的就是这里，写错了会被 Director CRITICAL。

### 2. 🖥️ 基础设施快照
  - 服务器名 + 工作目录 + Python 路径 + conda env
  - 模型路径 / 数据集路径 / wandb config / HF cache
  - 活跃进程：PID + tmux session + cron 任务 ID
  写一次就稳定，下一个"你"不用再 SSH 探查。

### 3. 📊 当前状态
  - 你刚做完什么 / 正在做什么
  - registry 里 RUNNING/PREPARING 的实验列表 + 一句话摘要
  - 等待中的 Worker 回调 / Cron / Director 决策

### 4. 📋 待办（条件触发式）
  格式 "条件 X 满足 → 动作 Y"。例如:
  - "Falcon-H1R 下载完成 → sed 修 MODEL_PATH → dry-run → 提交"
  - "pilot_011 出 GC4 结果 → ≥2pp 转架构 narrative，<2pp 写 null-result"

### 5. 🎯 战略上下文
  - 项目核心结论 / 决策链（D001..）
  - 下一个"你"最容易判断错的事 / 容易踩的坑

**写作原则**：
- 第 1 节必须是项目特定的（不是通用 L1 红线）
- 第 2 节让下一轮 BOOT 后能立即开工，避免重新 SSH 探查
- 简洁 > 完整：每节 5-10 行足够，长 handoff 不等于好 handoff

示例格式:
&lt;handoff>
### 1. 🔴 硬约束 / 红线
- 本项目只能用 cuda:4 / cuda:5（之前两次违规 cuda:0 被 Director CRITICAL）
- submit_training_job 不支持 shell builtin（source/conda activate 全不行）

### 2. 🖥️ 基础设施快照
- 服务器：westd-8x6000，工作目录 /root/autodl-tmp/hybrid_reasoning_phases
- Python：/root/miniconda3/bin/python（绝对路径，不走 conda activate）
- 模型：/root/autodl-tmp/qwen35_4b_direct
- 活跃：pilot_011 (PID 203221, tmux=pilot_011, cuda:4)

### 3. 📊 当前状态
我刚完成 pilot_010 分析，正在等 pilot_011 (Falcon-H1R) CoT 生成完成（6/50）。
registry: pilot_011 RUNNING；其他全 DONE。

### 4. 📋 待办
- pilot_011 cron 回调 → spawn worker 跑 probe + GC4 计算
- GC4 ≥2pp → 转架构 narrative；<2pp → null-result paper outline 已就绪

### 5. 🎯 战略上下文
Falcon-H1R 是最后一个数据点。已知 4B/9B GC4 都 FAIL。
易错：不要把 GC4-par (Falcon parallel) 和 GC4-seq (Qwen sequential) 直接对比。
&lt;/handoff>
